Lesson 24: Pass protection: count blockers vs. rushers (and why sacks happen)

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked tonight and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files, so there are no misses to fix. This lesson adds a new concept that Thursday's game will put on display: Seattle's line has allowed 8 sacks in two games, and Denver's defense just sacked Justin Herbert 5 times.

The big idea: on every pass play, the offense decides how many players stay back to block, and the defense decides how many to send. Compare the two numbers and you know what kind of pass is coming, and whether a sack is likely.

Picture A and B match the numbered points below. White circles = blockers; X = rushers; yellow X = the rusher nobody blocks.

  1. Five always block. The five offensive linemen block on every pass. The offense can add a running back (6 blockers) or a tight end too (7, "max protection"). Every extra blocker is one fewer receiver.
  2. Picture A: more rushers than blockers. Six rushers against five blockers means one is free. The quarterback has to get rid of the ball fast to the receiver the blitzer left open (the "hot" throw), or he gets sacked. This is the blitz gamble from Lesson 6, seen from the offense's side.
  3. Picture B: more blockers than rushers. Seven blockers against four rushers gives the quarterback time, often 3 seconds or more, so the play is usually a deep throw or play-action (Lesson 16). The cost: only three receivers, so the defense can double the best one.
  4. Quick throws are protection too. A team with a weak line can throw short and fast instead. On Oct 4, the 49ers got zero sacks on Bo Nix even though Denver threw 42 times, because his passes traveled only 34 air yards in total: the ball was gone before the rush got there.
  5. Pressure turns into runs. When rushers fly upfield and the coverage turns its back (man coverage, Lesson 5), running lanes open for the quarterback. Monday, Josh Allen ran 10 times for 121 yards, several of them scrambles from pass plays.

Real examples:

  • Sunday: 49ers 32, Seahawks 29. San Francisco sacked Sam Darnold 5 times (Gracen Halton 1.5) and forced 2 interceptions. Darnold has been sacked 8 times in two games: Seattle's line keeps ending up in picture A.
  • Sunday: Broncos at Chargers. Denver sacked Justin Herbert 5 times and scored all 13 of its second-half points off Chargers turnovers. Pressure leads to rushed throws, and rushed throws lead to turnovers.
  • Thursday's question (Fri 9:15 a.m. JST / Thu 5:15 p.m. PT): does Seattle keep its tight end and back in to block (picture B, fewer targets for Jaxon Smith-Njigba) or throw quickly to beat the rush? Either way, it's why the brief likes Denver getting points, and why Kalshi's "Darnold 5+ rushing yards" is on the props list.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 19 counted the box to predict run or pass. This lesson counts again after you know it's a pass: who stays home, and who rushes. Lesson 6 (blitz) is the defense adding rushers; Lesson 16 (play-action) is what an offense does with picture B's extra time.

For your Film Room drill: two pre-snap clues tell you about protection before the ball is snapped. No running back in the backfield (empty) = only five blockers = almost always a quick pass: lean PASS. A tight end lined up next to the tackle plus a back beside the QB on 3rd-and-long = room for seven blockers: lean PASS, and expect a deep throw. Note which one you saw with your call, so the review can check it.

Video: search YouTube for "NFL pass protection explained" and "hot route vs blitz explained."

Practice it in Football Camp → (Station 7: Read the snap)

Lesson 25: When the best cornerback is out: who covers the star receiver

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked tonight and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files (and the original football-film-room repository isn't reachable from this run). So this lesson adds a new concept that tonight's game is built around: Denver will play Seattle (Fri 9:15 a.m. JST / Thu 5:15 p.m. PT) without its best cornerback, Pat Surtain II, or starting safety Talanoa Hufanga, and Seattle's Jaxon Smith-Njigba has 100+ receiving yards in 4 of 5 games.

The big idea: every defense has to answer one question about the other team's best receiver: who covers him? With a star cornerback, the answer is easy: he follows that receiver everywhere ("shadows" him), one on one, and the other ten defenders play normally. Take the star corner away and the defense has two choices, and each one costs something.

White = offense, yellow = defense. WR wide receiver ("star WR" is the offense's best; "other WR" is the next one) · CB cornerback · S safety · QB quarterback · white squares = offensive linemen. Dashed white arrow = the star's route. Yellow arrow = the safety moving over before or at the snap.

  1. Picture A, backup alone: the next cornerback covers the star one-on-one and the safety (S) stays in the middle of the field. Denver's options are Riley Moss (questionable, ribs) and Josh Jobe (PFF grade 54.1 this season). The cost: the offense's best player gets single coverage from a weaker defender all game.
  2. Picture B, bracket (two on one): the cornerback plays underneath (takes the short throw) and a safety rotates over the top (takes the deep throw), so the star has a defender on both sides. The cost: that safety has left the middle, so the other receiver ("other WR") is one-on-one and the middle of the field is open for runs and tight ends.
  3. How you spot it before the snap: it's Lesson 20 (read the safeties) with one more question. Two safeties split evenly = no special help. Two safeties, but one shaded toward the star's side = a bracket is likely: the defense just told you who it fears.
  4. Why it decides games: once the star is bracketed, the offense's second and third options have to win. Seattle's are thin (Smith-Njigba has about half of Seattle's receiving yards, per TheSpread). That's why the brief still likes Denver +3.5 without Surtain: Picture B makes Seattle beat Denver with someone else.

Real examples:

  • Picture A gone wrong: USC 31, Penn State 23 (Oct 10). Penn State played without three cornerbacks, so it had nobody to bracket with. Jayden Maiava threw for 301 yards and 3 TDs (210 by halftime) and Trent Mosley caught 9 for 111.
  • A different choice: Bills 37, Rams 34 (Oct 12). Buffalo played zone about 78% of the time instead of doubling Puka Nacua. He caught a Monday-night-record 17 passes on 24 targets, but for 138 yards, about 8 a catch: zone gave up the short catches and took away the big ones. Defenses choose which damage to accept.
  • Tonight's tell: on Seattle's first third down, does a Denver safety drift toward Smith-Njigba before the snap (Picture B), or stay centered (Picture A)? VSiN expects Denver to load up against the run instead, which would mean more Picture A, the best case for Smith-Njigba.

Connecting the dots: shadowing is Lesson 5's man coverage aimed at one player; a bracket is a Cover 2 idea (Lesson 8) used on one side of the field; Lesson 9's bunched receivers are how offenses make brackets hard (who does the safety help on when three receivers stand together?). And Lesson 19's box count tells you what the defense gave up to send that safety over.

For your Film Room drill: before each snap, find the offense's best receiver and the nearest safety. Safety shaded toward him + six or fewer in the box = the defense chose the bracket and gave up the run: lean RUN (or a throw to the other side). Safety centered, the star split wide by himself, passing down = one-on-one: lean PASS, toward the star. Log "bracket" or "1-on-1" with your call, so the review can check it.

Video: search YouTube for "bracket coverage explained NFL" and "shadow cornerback explained."

Practice it in Football Camp → (Station 3: Safeties & coverages)

Lesson 26: Overtime: how NFL and college OT work (and why it counts for your bets)

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked tonight and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files (the original football-film-room repository isn't reachable from this run). So there's no miss of yours to fix, and this lesson takes a new concept from last night instead: both of Thursday's games went to overtime, and they used two completely different sets of rules. It also matters for betting: UAB-ECU's total went from 34 to 47 in overtime.

The big idea: overtime is a mini-game with its own rules. In the NFL, both teams get a chance with the ball (in the regular season since 2025), then the next score wins, inside a 10-minute clock. In college there is no clock at all: each team gets one possession from the opponent's 25-yard line, over and over, until one side is ahead after both have had the ball.

Left: Thursday's Broncos-Seahawks overtime. Right: Thursday's UAB-East Carolina overtime. Each box is one step; the yellow box ended the game. PAT = point after touchdown (extra point). INT = interception.

  1. NFL, step by step (left): a coin toss decides who gets the ball. Seattle won it and drove, but Ja'Quan McMillian intercepted Sam Darnold on 3rd-and-7. Under the old rule, a Seattle field goal on that first drive would have let Denver answer, but a Seattle touchdown would have ended it; now even a first-drive touchdown gives the other team a turn. Once both teams have had the ball, the next score of any kind wins: Denver's drive ended with Wil Lutz's 53-yard field goal, 17-14.
  2. If nobody scores in 10 minutes, an NFL regular-season game ends in a tie, which is how Giants-Commanders finished 27-27 last Sunday (the brief's Giants +3.5 won on it).
  3. College, step by step (right): UAB went first from ECU's 25 and scored (Ryder Burton's 6-yard TD to Rod Robinson II, plus the extra point: 24-17). ECU then had to score a touchdown from UAB's 25 to keep playing. Emory Williams threw an interception and Delvon Gulley ran it back 80 yards. ECU's possession was over, so the game was over, and the return touchdown still counted: 30-17.
  4. College's 2-point rules: from the 2nd overtime, a team that scores a touchdown must go for 2 (no kick). From the 3rd overtime on, there are no drives at all, just one 2-point play per team, back and forth. That's why long college overtimes end with scores like 61-58.
  5. Why the coin toss choice differs: in college, the toss winner almost always chooses defense first, so it knows exactly what it needs when its turn comes (the recaps don't say who won Thursday's toss; UAB going first suggests ECU made that choice). In the NFL, taking the ball first is usually right now, because you're guaranteed a second possession if the game is still tied after both teams have had it.

Real examples:

  • Bills 37, Rams 34 (Oct 12): Buffalo got the ball first in overtime, and James Cook fumbled at the Rams' 20. The Rams drove to Buffalo's 30, but Greg Rousseau blocked Harrison Mevis' 48-yard try. Both teams had now had the ball, so Tyler Bass's 45-yarder on the next drive ended it. Same rule as Thursday.
  • Why the NFL changed the rule: in the January 2022 playoffs, the Chiefs beat the Bills 42-36 on the first overtime drive and Josh Allen never touched the ball. The league guaranteed both teams a possession in the playoffs the next year, and in the regular season starting in 2025.
  • Betting, from UAB-ECU: the total was 47.5. Regulation ended 17-17 (34 points); overtime added 13; final 47, so the Under won by half a point. UAB +4.5 won too. Sportsbooks count overtime for spreads, totals and player props, and Kalshi's game, spread and total markets follow the official final (check the rules line on any contract). An Under bettor at 34 points with the score tied is not safe yet.

Connecting the dots: overtime is the two-minute drill (Lessons 11 and 23) with no clock in college, and the red zone (Lesson 12) every time: college OT starts at the 25, just outside it. Kicking matters more than anywhere else (Lesson 4): Lutz's 53-yarder won Thursday; Mevis' blocked 48-yarder lost Monday.

For your Film Room drill: in overtime, ask what the offense needs before you call run or pass. Team going second in college, down 7: it can't kick, so long downs lean PASS and 4th down is a go. NFL team that only needs a field goal, already in range: lean RUN (protect the ball, center it for the kicker), like Denver's drive before Lutz's kick. Log "OT: needs TD" or "OT: needs FG" with your call.

Video: search YouTube for "NFL overtime rules 2025 explained" and "college football overtime rules explained."

Practice it in Football Camp → (Station 6: Situations)

Lesson 27: Backup quarterbacks: how the game plan shrinks (and how to call it in your drill)

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked tonight and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files, and the original football-film-room repository isn't reachable from this run. So there's no miss of yours to fix, and this lesson takes a new concept straight from this weekend: backup quarterbacks are starting everywhere. Friday, Washington won at Purdue with its backup. This weekend: Tanner McKee for Jalen Hurts (Eagles), Tyler Huntley for Lamar Jackson (Ravens), Jalon Daniels for Baker Mayfield (Bucs), maybe Tyson Bagent for Caleb Williams (Bears), and in college Ty Dieffenbach (UCLA), George MacIntyre (Tennessee) and Trammel Jones (Florida).

The big idea: a coach doesn't hand a backup the starter's whole playbook. The backup has had fewer practice snaps with the first team, and the coach trusts him less with risky throws. So the plan shrinks: more runs, more quick, short throws the quarterback can make on his first read, and fewer deep shots and long-developing plays. For your drill, that means a backup shifts your run/pass calls toward RUN on early downs.

Left: a starter's menu reaches all three passing depths (dashed = passes) plus normal runs (solid). Right: with a backup, thick arrows = used more (runs and quick short throws), thin dotted = used less (middle and deep). This is a schematic of the usual pattern, not a measured chart; the real examples below show it.

  1. More runs (bottom of the right panel): handing off is the safest play a backup can run, and it keeps the defense from teeing off on him. Last week (Thu Oct 8) Tampa started Jalon Daniels at Dallas and ran for 241 yards in a 24-16 upset. Last Sunday Baltimore started Tyler Huntley at Atlanta and ran the ball behind Derrick Henry: 111 rushing yards by halftime, and Baltimore had the ball 19:28 to Atlanta's 10:32 in the first half.
  2. Quick, safe throws (short zone): passes that come out in about two seconds to the first receiver he looks at (slants, hitches, screens: Lesson 16's play-action helps too). These cut down sacks and interceptions, which is what the coach fears most.
  3. Fewer middle and deep throws (top zones): throws over the middle and deep need the quarterback to read safeties (Lesson 20) and trust timing with his receivers. Backups get fewer of those calls, so the defense can creep closer to the line, which is why a backup's team often struggles to run even while trying to run more.
  4. When it breaks the pattern: some backups get the full menu and play well, and the defense baits them into mistakes anyway. Friday, Washington's Elijah Brown (starting for the injured Demond Williams Jr.) threw for 302 yards and 3 TDs on 28 passes, and also 3 interceptions. Washington still won 38-26 because Trey Cooley ran 8 times for 113 yards (runs of 43 and 53) and Purdue couldn't run at all.
  5. Why the betting market cares: the quarterback is the biggest single swing in a point spread. When Hurts was ruled out (a blood clot in his calf) along with Saquon Barkley, Eagles-Panthers flipped from Eagles -1.5 to Panthers -3.5 and the total fell to about 39. Mississippi State-LSU moved from LSU -10.5 to -15.5 once QB Kamario Taylor was listed doubtful. Backups also pull totals down: fewer deep shots, more clock-eating runs.

Real examples to watch this weekend:

  • Tennessee's George MacIntyre vs. Alabama (Sun 4:30 a.m. JST / Sat 12:30 p.m. PT, ABC): NBC/Yahoo's pick is the Under because both teams are expected to lean on the run.
  • UCLA's Ty Dieffenbach vs. Wisconsin (Sun 8:30 a.m. JST / Sat 4:30 p.m. PT, NBC): Nico Iamaleava is out for the season; the line dropped from UCLA -3.5 to -2.5.
  • Eagles' Tanner McKee vs. Carolina (Mon 2:00 a.m. JST / Sun 10:00 a.m. PT, CBS) and Ravens' Tyler Huntley at Cleveland (same time, FOX): count how often each team runs on 1st down in the first quarter.
  • It helps the other team's running back too: if the Bears start Tyson Bagent instead of Caleb Williams, Atlanta is likelier to lead late, and a team that leads runs, which is part of why the brief picked Bijan Robinson 70+ this week.

Connecting the dots: this is game script (Lesson 23) chosen before kickoff instead of forced by the score. It shows up in the box count (Lesson 19): defenses put an 8th player near the line against a backup because they fear the run more than the pass. And it ties to pass protection (Lesson 24): quick throws are how a coach protects a backup without extra blockers.

For your Film Room drill: before the first snap, note whether a backup is starting (the broadcast always says so). Then: 1st and 10 or 2nd and medium → lean RUN more than you normally would; 3rd and 7+ → still PASS (even a backup has to throw then, usually short, at or just past the sticks); leading in the 2nd half → RUN. Watch the first two drives: if the backup completes a couple of throws past 15 yards, the coach will open the playbook and you should move back toward your normal calls. Log "backup QB" in your notes so the review can check the pattern.

Video: search YouTube for "Tyler Huntley Ravens Falcons highlights 2026" and "Elijah Brown Washington Purdue highlights."

Practice it in Football Camp → (Station 8: The drill)

Lesson 28: The strip-sack: why one swipe at the ball decided Saturday's biggest games

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked this morning and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files (the original football-film-room repository isn't reachable from this run). So there's no miss of yours to fix, and this lesson takes a new concept straight from Saturday's noon games, where a strip-sack or a sack-fumble showed up in three of the four big finals.

The big idea: a strip-sack is a sack where the rusher knocks the ball out instead of (or while) tackling the quarterback. It almost always comes from the quarterback's blind side, the side he isn't facing when he sets up to throw: for a right-handed quarterback, that's his left. The rusher who gets there is usually the edge rusher lined up outside the left tackle. The ball is easiest to knock loose at one moment: when the quarterback has one hand on it with his arm cocked back to throw.

Top-down view, offense moving up the screen. The edge rusher (yellow X) starts outside the left tackle on the blind side, loops around him (dashed path) and arrives behind the quarterback, reaching for the ball in his right hand. Bottom boxes: when the ball is exposed vs. safe.

  1. Why the blind side (left of the picture): a right-handed quarterback drops back facing slightly right, so he can't see a rusher coming from his left. He never braces or tucks the ball. That's why the left tackle protecting that side is usually one of the best-paid players on an offense (Lesson 24 counted blockers vs. rushers; this is the one matchup inside that count that matters most).
  2. Why the arm cocked back (EXPOSED box): in the pocket, a good quarterback holds the ball with both hands, high and tight (SAFE box). To throw, he has to take one hand off and pull the ball back behind his ear. For that split second it's held by one hand, away from his body. A rusher arriving from behind doesn't tackle first: he chops or swipes down at that arm.
  3. Why it swings games (bottom line): a loose ball bouncing on the turf is close to a coin flip for who gets it, and the defense often recovers near the quarterback, which is deep in the offense's own territory. So one swipe turns a normal drive into a short field for the other team (Market 101 Lesson 9: turnovers and short fields).

Saturday's real examples:

  • Ohio State 19, Indiana 15 (Sat 9:00 a.m. PT): Indiana had one turnover all season, then lost fumbles on its first two drives of the second half. The second one was Josh Hoover sacked and fumbling in Ohio State territory, a scoring chance turned into an Ohio State possession. Ohio State won by 4.
  • Florida 27, Texas 24: Florida sacked Arch Manning 5 times, and the game ended when Myles Graham forced a Manning fumble with 1:58 left (Kanye Clark recovered) while Texas was trying to tie or win it. A three-point game, decided by one swipe.
  • Northwestern 24, Michigan State 21: Northwestern's Michael Kilbane strip-sacked the quarterback and recovered it himself, in a game won on a field goal with 5 seconds left.
  • Alabama at Tennessee (live): Alabama's Keelon Russell lost a fumble on a sack in the first half; a return touchdown was overturned (ruled down), but Tennessee kept the ball.
  • Watch Sunday: Chicago's pass rush is thin (Montez Sweat suspended, Dayo Odeyingbo on IR) against Michael Penix Jr. (ribs), Mon 2:00 a.m. JST (Sun 10:00 a.m. PT), FOX; and Tanner McKee, a backup who completes 41.9% under pressure per Sharp Football, faces Carolina, same time, CBS.

Connecting the dots: pass protection (Lesson 24) decides whether a rusher gets free; this lesson is what happens when one does. A backup quarterback (Lesson 27) holds the ball longer while reading, which is more time with the ball exposed, so coaches give backups quick throws. And a turnover creates a sudden change: the new offense starts on a short field, which takes you straight into the red zone (Lesson 12).

For your Film Room drill: (1) On 3rd and long, check the edge rusher outside the left tackle: if the defense puts its best pass rusher there and the offense leaves the left tackle alone with no tight end or back to help, expect a quick PASS (the coach knows the clock is short). (2) After a strip-sack or any takeaway, the first play for the new offense is often a deep shot, frequently play-action (Lesson 16): the defense that just gave up the ball is rattled and the coach wants to strike. Call PASS on that first snap and log "sudden change" in your notes so the review can check it.

Video: search YouTube for "Myles Graham forced fumble Arch Manning Florida Texas 2026" and "Josh Hoover fumble Ohio State Indiana 2026."

Lesson 29: Kicks are live balls: muffed punts, blocked punts and the onside kick

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked this morning and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files, and the original football-film-room repository was not reachable from this run (access denied). So there's no miss of yours to fix, and this lesson takes a new concept from the weekend, when a muffed punt, a blocked punt and a recovered onside kick each changed a game.

The big idea: on a normal play only the offense has the ball. On a kick, the ball is in the air between two teams, and the rules decide who is allowed to grab it. Most of the time the receiving team simply catches it. But a kick that is touched and dropped, blocked, or kicked short on purpose becomes a loose ball that either team can win, and that's a turnover that doesn't show up as one in the offense's stats.

Top, an onside kick seen from above: white = the kicking team (K kicker, KT kicking-team players) at its own 35; yellow = the receiving team's hands team (HT) just past the 45. The yellow line is the 10-yard mark. Bottom: three punt rules.

  1. The onside kick (top). The kicker (K) kicks the ball along the ground on purpose, short. In college it must travel 10 yards (from the own 35 to the 45, the yellow line) before the kicking team (KT) may recover it, unless a receiving player touches it first. The receiving team sends out its hands team (HT): receivers and backs who catch for a living. Most onside kicks fail, and a team that recovers one gets the ball near midfield. In the NFL a team must announce an onside kick, and only a team that's behind may try one.
  2. The muff (bottom left). A punt returner who touches the ball without catching it makes it a live ball, and the punting team can recover it. They get it where they recover it (they can't run with it). That's why returners let bouncing punts go: touching it is the mistake.
  3. The fair catch (bottom middle). A returner who waves one hand above his head can't be hit; the ball is dead where he catches it. It gives up the return to protect the ball.
  4. The blocked punt (bottom right). A punt blocked behind the line of scrimmage is a loose ball that either team can pick up and run with, like a fumble. A punter is usually standing 13–15 yards deep, so a block often happens deep in the punting team's territory.

This weekend's real examples:

  • Bears 31, Falcons 23 (Mon 2:00 a.m. JST / Sun 10:00 a.m. PT): Atlanta returner Zachariah Branch muffed a punt just before halftime; Chicago recovered and scored to make it 21-7. That lead is what took the ball away from Bijan Robinson (9 carries) and lost the brief's 70+ pick. Branch fumbled a second punt later.
  • Titans 26, Colts 20: down 13-0 at the half, Tennessee scored 20 in the 4th quarter, and one of the TDs was Femi Oladejo's 14-yard return of a blocked punt. Tennessee's first win of the season.
  • Michigan 21, Penn State 18 (Sun 4:30 a.m. JST / Sat 12:30 p.m. PT): after Rocco Becht's 24-yd TD to Chase Sowell with 1:11 left, Penn State needed an onside kick. Michigan's hands team recovered it at its own 49, and a Jordan Marshall 8-yard run got the first down that ended the game. The brief's Penn State +6.5 still won.
  • Florida 27, Texas 24 (Saturday): Ryan Niblett's 64-yard punt-return TD was 7 of Florida's 27 points in a 3-point upset.
  • Watch Monday: Commanders at 49ers, Tue 9:15 a.m. JST (Mon 5:15 p.m. PT), ESPN. If Washington is close late, the onside kick and the hands team are part of the brief's Commanders +7.5 math.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 4 introduced special teams and Lesson 15 the kickoff rules; this lesson is what happens when the ball comes loose. A recovered kick is a sudden change like the strip-sack (Lesson 28): the new offense starts on a short field, often already in the red zone (Lesson 12). And a failed onside kick hands the leading team the clock: that's the four-minute offense (Lesson 23).

For your Film Room drill: (1) After a failed onside kick the leading team runs to burn clock: call RUN on the next snaps (Michigan ran on every play after recovering). (2) After a muffed punt or blocked punt recovered near the goal line (inside the 10), call RUN (short yardage, Lesson 17); recovered near midfield, the first snap is often a PASS shot, as after a strip-sack. Log "sudden change" in your notes so the review can check it.

Video: search YouTube for "Zachariah Branch muffed punt Bears Falcons 2026" and "Femi Oladejo blocked punt touchdown Titans Colts."

Lesson 30: The tight end: half lineman, half receiver, and the matchup defenses can't solve

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked this morning and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files, and the original football-film-room repository was not reachable from this run (access denied: not configured for this session). So there's no miss of yours to fix. This lesson takes a new concept from Week 6, when tight ends scored or set up the big moments in four games, and from tonight's Monday-night game, where George Kittle is the biggest risk to the brief's Commanders +7.5.

The big idea: the tight end (TE) lines up at the end of the offensive line, so he can block like a sixth lineman or run out for a pass like a receiver. The defense can't tell which from where he stands, and it has no player who matches him well at both jobs: a linebacker is big enough to take on his block but usually too slow to cover him; a safety can run with him but gives up 30–60 pounds.

White = offense (squares = linemen LT, LG, C, RG, RT; Y = inline tight end; TE = flexed tight end; WR receivers; QB; RB). Yellow = defense (DL linemen, LB linebackers, CB cornerbacks, S safeties). Dashed line = line of scrimmage; solid arrow = a block, dashed arrow = a pass route.

  1. Where he lines up. The inline TE (Y) stands right next to the right tackle (RT), usually in a 3-point stance (one hand on the ground) like a lineman. The flexed TE stands a yard or two off the line and outside, upright like a receiver. Some teams split a TE all the way out wide just to see who covers him.
  2. Job 1: block the edge. On an outside run, the inline TE blocks the defensive end (the DL across from him) so the running back can get around the corner. In pass protection he often "chips" that rusher (one quick hit) before running his route: the extra blocker you counted in Lesson 24.
  3. Job 2: the seam route. The flexed TE runs straight up the field between the linebacker and the safety, the "seam." The LB: too slow to stay with him; the S: too small to stop him at the catch. Against zone he sits in the soft spot between them (Lesson 8).
  4. Personnel tells you how many there are (bottom boxes). Lesson 1's numbers: 11 personnel = 1 TE, 12 = 2 TEs, 13 = 3. More tight ends usually make the defense bring in bigger players (base defense, Lesson 2), and that's exactly when play-action (Lesson 16) to a tight end works best.
  5. The red zone. Inside the 20 the field shrinks (Lesson 12), and a 6-foot-5 target who can box out a smaller defender is the easiest throw there. That's why tight ends score so many short touchdowns.

Week 6's real examples:

  • Kyle Pitts (Falcons): 5 catches, 60 yards and 2 TDs in the 31-23 loss to Chicago. Atlanta was throwing to catch up, the same script that sank the brief's Bijan Robinson 70+.
  • Tommy Tremble (Panthers): caught both of Bryce Young's TD passes in the 25-14 win at Philadelphia.
  • Colby Parkinson (Rams): the go-ahead TD from Matthew Stafford with 47 seconds left against Arizona (Arizona still won with a field goal at 0:00).
  • Michael Mayer and Brock Bowers (Raiders): 7-95 (a career high) and 4-70 in the 33-30 loss to Buffalo. Two tight ends on the field together is 12 personnel.
  • Tonight: George Kittle (49ers): 21 catches, 268 yards and 4 TDs through Week 5, and his 56-yard TD sealed the Week 4 win over Denver. Washington has allowed 7 TDs to tight ends; his prop is 63.5 receiving yards and Kalshi's anytime-TD price is about 47¢. With Deebo Samuel out and Mike Evans questionable, more throws should go his way: the main risk to the brief's Commanders +7.5, Tue 9:15 a.m. JST (Mon 5:15 p.m. PT), ESPN/ABC.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 1 (personnel) counts the tight ends; Lesson 10 (formations) shows where they stand; Lesson 19 (count the box) explains why an extra inline TE pulls an extra defender into the box; Lesson 22 (motion) is how offenses move the TE before the snap to create the mismatch.

For your Film Room drill: (1) Inline TE with a hand on the ground, especially two or three of them (12 or 13 personnel), in short yardage or the four-minute offense → lean RUN. (2) TE standing up, flexed off the line or split wide → lean PASS. (3) TE motions across the formation right before the snap → he's often going to block the back side of a run, or it's play-action: call RUN unless it's 3rd-and-long. Note the TE's stance next to each call so the review can check which tell you used.

Video: search YouTube for "George Kittle seam touchdown" and "Kyle Pitts touchdowns Bears Falcons 2026."

Lesson 31: Protecting a lead: why 20-3 at halftime became 23-17 (and why it matters for the points)

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked this morning and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files, and the original football-film-room repository was not reachable from this run (access denied: not configured for this session). So there's no miss of yours to fix. This lesson takes a new concept from Monday night, when the 49ers nearly lost a 20-point lead and the brief's Commanders +7.5 won on Washington's late touchdowns.

The big idea: once a team leads by two scores or more in the second half, both teams change how they play. The team that's ahead stops trying to score fast and starts trying to use up the clock: its offense runs the ball (the four-minute offense, Lesson 23) and its defense backs off so nothing goes over its head. The team that's behind throws on almost every play. Those two changes together are why big leads so often shrink late, even when the leading team was much better for most of the game.

White = offense (squares = linemen; WR receivers; QB). Yellow = defense (DL linemen, LB linebackers, CB cornerbacks, S safeties). Dashed white line = line of scrimmage; dashed arrows = pass routes. Bottom bars: white = 1st half, yellow = 2nd half.

  1. The deep zone (top band). A defense protecting a big lead puts four defensive backs 20 or more yards deep. Its only goal is to stop a one-play touchdown. A 12-yard catch in front of them is fine: the receiver gets tackled, and the clock keeps running. This is called a "prevent" or soft zone.
  2. Only 3 rushers. To have enough players deep, the defense often rushes just three linemen. With fewer rushers, the quarterback has more time (Lesson 24's counting: more blockers than rushers), so he can complete pass after pass.
  3. Short throws in front. The receivers' routes stop underneath, in the open space between the linebackers and the deep zone. Each one gains 8 to 15 yards. On Monday, nine different Commanders caught a pass and Jayden Daniels finished 29 of 40 for 300 yards, most of it after halftime.
  4. The leader's offense goes quiet. The team that's ahead runs into defenses that know a run is coming (an extra defender in the box, Lesson 19). Short runs, punts, and the other team gets the ball back. San Francisco's last three drives were three-and-outs that gained 7 yards in total.
  5. Yards by half (bottom bars). The 49ers had 303 yards in the first half and 72 in the second; Washington had 90, then 224. Same teams, same night: the score changed how both of them played.

Why it doesn't always work for the trailing team: it's racing the clock, so it needs everything to go right. Washington got help from four 49ers defensive penalties, but on 4th down from the SF 17 Malik Mustapha knocked the ball from Treylon Burks in the end zone, and the last snap was a sack. Leads shrink much more often than they disappear.

Real examples:

  • 49ers 23, Commanders 17 (Monday): 20-3 at the half, 23-3 with 18 seconds left in the 3rd, then two Daniels TD passes. Washington +7.5 covered by a point and a half.
  • Michigan 21, Penn State 18 (Saturday): Michigan led 14-3 after three quarters (two TDs off Penn State interceptions) and scored again in the 4th; Penn State scored 15 in the 4th (Carson Hansen's TD and 2-point pass, then Rocco Becht to Chase Sowell for 24 yards with 1:11 left). Michigan recovered the onside kick. Penn State +6.5 covered.
  • Bills 33, Raiders 30 (Sunday): Tre Tucker's 44-yard TD with 1:32 left made it a 3-point game; Las Vegas +4.5 to +5.5 covered.
  • The other way: Titans 26, Colts 20 (Sunday): Indianapolis led 13-0 at the half and lost; Tennessee scored 20 in the 4th, including a blocked-punt return TD. Sometimes the lead doesn't just shrink.

What it means for your bets: a late touchdown by a team that's losing anyway changes nothing about who wins, but it can flip the spread. Bettors call that a "backdoor cover." When you take the points (Commanders +7.5, Penn State +6.5), garbage-time scoring works for you; when you lay a big number with a favorite, it works against you. It also matters live: a 17-point halftime lead tends to be smaller by the end, so a live price that assumes the leader keeps scoring at the same rate is usually too high (Market 101, Lesson 8).

Connecting the dots: Lesson 23 (the clock changes the call) is the leading team's side of this; Lesson 24 (count blockers vs. rushers) explains why a 3-man rush gives the quarterback time; Lesson 8 (zone coverage) explains why the open space is underneath; Lesson 19 (count the box) is why the leader's runs stop working.

For your Film Room drill: (1) Team ahead by 10+ in the second half, clock running → lean RUN, even on 2nd and 8. (2) Team behind by 10+ in the 4th quarter → PASS on almost every down. (3) Defense shows 3 linemen with safeties very deep → it's sitting on a lead; expect a short pass underneath. Write the score and quarter next to each call so the review can check whether the situation, not the formation, made the call.

Video: search YouTube for "49ers Commanders highlights Monday Night Football 2026" and "Penn State Michigan 2026 highlights."

Lesson 32: The middle of the line: why backup guards matter more than you'd think

Why this lesson now: the Film Room mirror (football-brief, branch film-room) was checked this morning and still has no reviewed snaps: the only logged game, Eagles at Bears (Week 3), has 0 saved snaps and there are no review files, and the original football-film-room repository was not reachable from this run (access denied: not configured for this session). So there's no miss of yours to fix. This lesson takes a new concept from Thursday night: New England plays Chicago with its third- and fourth-string guards, and that's the football reason behind the brief's Under 50.5.

The big idea: fans watch the tackles, who block the fast pass rushers on the outside. But the two guards and the center protect the most important spot on the field: the space right in front of the quarterback. Pressure from the outside, the quarterback can escape by stepping forward. Pressure up the middle, he has nowhere to go. That's why a team can survive a backup tackle more easily than two backup guards.

White squares = offensive linemen (yellow squares = backup guards). Yellow dots = defenders. Solid yellow arrow = linebacker blitzing the A gap; dashed yellow arrow = defensive tackle looping on a twist. Dashed white line = line of scrimmage. Bottom: the Patriots' guard situation for Thursday night.

  1. The gaps (labels on the line). Every space between two linemen has a letter. The A gaps are on either side of the center; the B gaps are between each guard and tackle (Lesson 14 used the same letters for run defense). Guards are responsible for the A and B gaps, the shortest path to the quarterback.
  2. Step up (the dashed line behind the center). Against edge rushers, a quarterback "steps up" into the pocket, and the rushers run past him. That escape only works if the middle holds. A rusher through the A gap arrives in about 2 seconds and closes it, so the throw has to come out early or not at all.
  3. The twist, or stunt (dashed yellow arrow). Two defensive linemen swap lanes after the snap: one charges at a guard, the other loops behind him into the gap next door. Beating it takes two blockers who trust each other to "pass off" the defenders. Backup guards who haven't practiced together (and on a Thursday they've had 3 days, Market 101 Lesson 28) are the classic victims.
  4. The A-gap blitz (solid yellow arrow). A linebacker walks up over the center and runs straight through. If the center and guards are busy with the twist, nobody is left for him (Lesson 24's counting: more rushers than blockers in one spot).
  5. Who plays Thursday (bottom panel). New England has lost four guards: Mike Onwenu and Greg Van Roten (IR), Alijah Vera-Tucker (hamstring) and Ben Brown (knee), both ruled out Wednesday. Walter Rouse and Matt Pryor start. RT Morgan Moses is questionable, and rookie Caleb Lomu played 59 snaps at right tackle last week.

How offenses hide a weak middle: quick throws (the ball out before the twist gets home), screens (let the rushers in on purpose and throw over them), runs to the outside instead of up the middle, and keeping a tight end or running back in to block, which means one fewer receiver. Every one of those shrinks the playbook, which is the case for fewer points (and for an Under).

Real examples:

  • Patriots at Bears (Thursday): New England's backup guards against a Chicago defense that's top-10 in EPA per play and has 42 takeaways since the start of last season. Drake Maye has thrown 10 TDs and 1 INT in his last three starts, so this is the test of whether that holds with a patched middle.
  • The counterexample: Michigan 21, Penn State 18 (Saturday): Michigan played without OL Andrew Sprague and Ace Hamilton, with three more linemen questionable, and still ran for 211 yards. A weak line is a reason to expect trouble, not a guarantee, especially for runs, where the blockers move forward instead of holding their ground.
  • Steelers 26, Buccaneers 19 (Sunday): Aaron Rodgers was sacked 5 times; the last one, by Jalen Ramsey on 4th down in overtime, ended the game. Inside pressure on a quarterback who doesn't move much is the hardest kind to escape.

What it means for your bets: an injury report that lists two guards is easy to skip (nobody knows the names), which is why it can matter more than the price shows. Pass-heavy props for a quarterback behind backup guards get riskier; Unders get a reason. But check the other side too: Thursday's game has a reason for the Under (New England's guards) and a reason for the Over (New England's three missing cornerbacks). The brief's estimate is only a small edge because both are real.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 24 (count blockers vs. rushers) explains why an extra rusher in one gap gets free; Lesson 6 (the blitz) is the gamble the defense takes to make it happen; Lesson 14 (gaps) is the same map used for runs; Lesson 28 (the strip-sack) is what happens when pressure arrives before the quarterback can protect the ball.

For your Film Room drill: (1) Offense with backup interior linemen, 3rd and 6+ → expect a quick PASS (screen or short throw), not a deep drop-back. (2) Linebacker walked up over the center before the snap → blitz likely; the call is still PASS, but expect it out fast. (3) 1st and 10 with an extra tight end or back staying in → lean RUN or play-action, since the offense is adding blockers. Note the down, distance and who's playing guard next to each call so the review can check whether the line, not the formation, decided it.

Video: search YouTube for "offensive line stunt twist explained" and "A gap blitz explained NFL," then watch Thursday's Patriots-Bears for how often Maye throws within 2.5 seconds.
