Lesson archive

Market 101 lessons, oldest first. Football 101 lessons now live together in the course above.

Market 101 · Lesson 1: Implied probability and the book's cut

American odds are a win chance in disguise. For a favorite, divide the odds by the odds plus 100: Penn State at -420 is 420 / 520 = 80.8%. For an underdog, divide 100 by the odds plus 100: Wisconsin at +320 is 100 / 420 = 23.8%.

Those add to 104.6%. A fair coin can't be 52% heads and 52% tails, so the extra 4.6% is the sportsbook's built-in cut, called the vig. It's why you need to win more than half your bets just to break even.

On Kalshi, a contract at 81 cents means the market sees an 81% chance, and a correct contract pays $1. The fee is charged separately instead of hiding inside the price.

Next: the point spread, and why -110 is the standard price.

Market 101 · Lesson 2: The point spread, and why -110 is everywhere

A spread handicaps the favorite. "Packers -4.5" means Green Bay must win by 5 or more for that bet to cash. "Falcons +4.5" wins if Atlanta loses by 4 or fewer, or wins outright. The half point exists so the bet can't end in a tie, which is called a push.

The standard price on each side is -110: you risk $110 to win $100. Using Lesson 1's formula, 110 / 210 = 52.4%. So to break even betting spreads, you have to win 52.4% of the time, not 50%. That 2.4% is the vig at work.

On Kalshi, the same bet is a contract like "Packers win by 6+." If it trades at 51¢, you pay 51¢ to win $1, which beats -110's 52.4¢ equivalent before fees. That's the kind of gap the price board is hunting for.

Next: why lines move, and how to tell sharp money from public money.

Market 101 · Lesson 3: Why lines move

A sportsbook's opening line is its best guess. After that, three things move it:

News. Injuries and QB changes. Thursday's line dropped from 7 to 4.5 when Penix was confirmed as the starter and Green Bay's injuries piled up. That move turned out to be right; Atlanta won by 21.

Money, and whose money. Books care less about how much is bet than who bets it. Professional bettors ("sharps") have long track records of beating closing lines, so books move quickly when sharps bet. Casual bettors ("the public") tend to back favorites and Overs, and books often let that money sit.

Key numbers. Football margins bunch up at 3 and 7 because of field goals and touchdowns. A move from USC -1.5 to Oregon -3 isn't just 4.5 points; it crossed zero and landed on 3, which is a big statement. Books move off 3 reluctantly.

The tell: when most bets are on one team but the line moves the other way, that's called reverse line movement, and it usually means sharp money is on the other side.

Next: parlays, and why books love them.

Market 101 · Lesson 4: Parlays, and why books love them

A parlay ties several bets together: every leg has to win, and the payout multiplies. Two -110 legs pay about +264; three pay about +596. Big numbers, which is the appeal.

The catch is that the vig multiplies too. Each -110 leg needs a 52.4% win rate to break even, so two legs need 52.4% × 52.4% = 27.4%, and three need 14.4%. A true coin-flip bettor hits a two-leg parlay 25% of the time, below that break-even. That gap is why books earn a bigger cut on parlays than on straight bets.

When a parlay makes sense: when the legs are correlated, meaning one leg winning makes the other more likely. Thursday's Falcons +5.5 with the Under was correlated: a low-scoring game helps the underdog stay close. Today's Oklahoma and Iowa parlay isn't correlated, which is why it's sized at half a unit.

On Kalshi: combos are priced by multiplying the contract prices. Two 50¢ outcomes cost about 25¢ combined, so the same math applies, minus fees.

Next: bankroll and unit sizing, or how not to go broke being right.

Market 101 · Bonus: Kalshi combos: Four things to know

1. Kalshi's price is an offer, not a set formula. When you build a combo, other traders on Kalshi offer you a price. The "fair price" is just the individual prices multiplied together (56¢ × 55¢ = 30.8¢). Anything above that is extra cost to you, like the house's cut at a sportsbook.

2. Keep combos short. Two or three bets is best. The more you add, the fewer traders offer a price, and the worse the price gets.

3. Same-game combos (two bets on one game, like Combo 3) only work on games Kalshi has opened for it, which often happens close to kickoff.

4. No do-overs if a player sits. At a sportsbook, if a player in your parlay doesn't play, that piece usually just gets dropped. On Kalshi, it can instead settle for part of its value, which shrinks your payout. It's marked with a blue arrow in the app.

Market 101 · Lesson 5: Units: how not to go broke while being right

The idea: pick one amount as your "unit" and bet that same amount (or a set fraction of it) every time. Most people who track betting seriously use 1–2% of their total betting money as a unit. So if you set aside $500 for the season, a unit is $5 to $10.

Why it matters: even good bettors lose often. Someone who wins 55% of their bets, which is very good, will still hit losing streaks of 5 or 6 in a row. If you bet big after a loss to "win it back," one bad streak can wipe you out. Same-size bets let a good record actually show up in your results.

How the brief uses it: 1 unit for the picks I like most, half a unit for parlays and combos (they lose more often), and a quarter unit for long shots. This morning is a good example: the Chase TD was only a quarter unit, but it still paid 0.33 units because it was priced at 43¢.

A simple rule: never raise your unit because you're behind. Raise it only after a long stretch of good results, and lower it if your total pot shrinks.

Next: "closing line value," or how to tell whether you're betting well even when you lose.

Market 101 · Lesson 6: Closing line value: were you right before the game even started?

The idea: the "closing line" is the last price before kickoff. By then every injury report, weather update and big bettor has pushed the price to its most accurate point. If you consistently buy at a better price than the close, you're beating the market, even on bets that lose.

Your weekend, as an example: you bought Davante Adams to score at about 46¢. Earlier Sunday he was 43¢, and after Nacua was ruled out the fair price should have been higher. If Adams closed at, say, 50¢, you "beat the close" by 4¢. The bet still lost, but the decision was good. Do that 100 times and the wins show up.

The other direction: the Vikings add-on at about 56¢ came right after Tampa scored, when the price had already moved up. Buying after a move usually means paying the new, less generous price.

How to use it: screenshot the Kalshi price when you buy, then check it again right before kickoff. Keep a simple tally: "beat the close" or "didn't." Over a season, that tally predicts your results better than your win-loss record does.

Next: why "the public" loves favorites and overs, and how to fade them.

Market 101 · Lesson 7: Why popular teams cost extra on Kalshi, and how to use it

The idea in one line: a Kalshi price is set by whoever is buying, not by a bookmaker. When lots of casual fans pile onto a famous, popular team, they push its price higher than its real chance of winning. That extra is called a "public premium."

How to spot it (three steps):

  1. Get the sportsbook price and remove the cut. Example from Monday: the Eagles closed at -186 and the Bears at +178. Those turn into 65.0% and 36.0%, which add to 101% (the extra 1% is the book's cut from Lesson 1). Divide each by 101% to get the fair numbers: Eagles about 64%, Bears about 36%.
  2. Compare to Kalshi. Kalshi had the Eagles at 69¢, about 5¢ above fair.
  3. Flip it. If one side is 5¢ too expensive, the other side is about that much too cheap. The Bears were roughly 31–32¢ on Kalshi against a fair ~36¢.

What happened: the Bears won 27-7. One game proves nothing; the point is that you'd have been paying less than fair. Do that over and over and it adds up, exactly like the "beat the close" idea from Lesson 6.

Where the premium usually shows up: prime-time games (everyone's watching), famous teams (Eagles, Cowboys, Chiefs, Ohio State, Alabama), "Yes" on overs and touchdown scorers, and the favorite in a game with a big-name QB. Fans rarely rush to buy "Under" or "No."

This week's example: Kalshi has the Steelers at 59¢ Thursday vs. about 57% fair at the sportsbooks. That's only a 2¢ gap, which is noise by the rule below, so the brief's Browns lean rests on football reasons, not price.

The rule of thumb: a 1–2¢ gap is noise. At 3¢ or more, lean toward the cheaper side, and only if the football reasons agree.

Next: live betting. When a mid-game price is worth buying and when it's just a reaction to one play.

Market 101 · Lesson 8: Live betting: when a mid-game price is a deal, and when it's just a reaction

The idea: Kalshi keeps trading during the game, so prices jump after every big play. A live bet is worth it only when the price moved more than the play actually changed the game.

Ask two questions before buying live:

  1. Did the play change who's better, or just the score? A fluky touchdown (a tipped interception, a return TD) changes the score but not which team is stronger. An injury to a starting QB changes both.
  2. How much time is left? Early in a game, a 7-point lead is worth much less than it feels. A team down 7 in the 1st quarter still wins roughly a third of the time.

Real example: Monday night, the Eagles were down 7-0 early. Their price dropped, but nothing about the teams had changed. That's the kind of "dip" that can be a buy. Then the Eagles kept turning the ball over and lost 27-7. The dip was reasonable to buy, but turnovers that keep happening are a sign something real is wrong.

Your own history: last Sunday's Vikings add-on came right after Tampa scored. The price had already moved by then, so you paid the new price instead of catching the swing.

Rules for tonight: (1) Decide before kickoff what you'd buy and at what price; write it down. (2) Only buy after a play that's fluky. (3) Never live-bet to win back a loss from earlier the same night.

Next: how weather and wind really affect totals (and when they don't).

Market 101 · Lesson 9: Why "low-scoring" bets fail: turnovers and short fields

The idea: a total (over/under) is really a bet on how many times each team gets a good chance to score. Weather, defense and pace matter, but the one thing nobody can predict, turnovers, can wreck an under in a single quarter.

Why turnovers matter so much: after an interception or fumble, the other offense often starts near midfield or closer instead of at its own 25. Shorter fields mean easier points (Football 101, Lesson 12). Thursday's Steelers-Browns game had 5 turnovers (4 interceptions, 1 lost fumble) and 5 sacks, and a game priced at 37.5 points finished with 51.

The weather lesson from the same night: the brief said it would add the under only if rain and wind held. The forecast cleared, the brief didn't add it on its own, and that was the right call. But it kept the under inside a combo, so it still lost there. When the reason for a bet goes away, drop the bet everywhere, including combos.

How to use it:

  1. Unders need careful QBs. Before betting an under, check each QB's interceptions and fumbles. Watson and Rodgers both throw picks; that should have been a warning.
  2. Overtime counts. Most totals include overtime, so a tied game late can blow an under that was safe. North Texas-Tulsa was already over in regulation, but OT added 27 more points.
  3. Prefer team totals. If you think one offense will struggle, bet that team's points under instead of the whole game. The other team's turnovers don't hurt you as much.

Next: reading injury reports: which "questionable" tags actually move a line.

Market 101 · Lesson 10: Three-leg combos: how the odds shrink, using your bet as the example

The rule: to get a combo's chance of winning, multiply the legs' chances together. Every leg you add makes the whole thing much less likely.

Your combo, built one leg at a timeChanceFair payout on $4.99
Michigan wins69%$7.23
+ Mississippi State wins69% × 35% = 24%$20.66
+ Michigan State wins24% × 24% = 5.8%$86.09
What Kalshi paid youimplied 6.2%$80.77

What that means: you'd win this about once in 17 tries. The $5.32 gap between the fair $86.09 and your $80.77 is Kalshi's cut (about 6%). That's normal for a combo, but notice it's bigger than the cut on a single bet. The more legs, the bigger the cut you pay.

Are the legs connected? Combos are only a good deal when one leg winning makes another more likely (Lesson 4's "correlated" idea, like an underdog plus the under). These three games have nothing to do with each other, so there's no hidden edge. It's a pure long shot.

When a combo like this makes sense: as a small, fun swing, sized at a quarter unit ($5) or less, which is exactly what you did. Where it goes wrong is when long-shot combos start to be the main bets; at 1-in-17, a long losing streak is normal even if every leg was fairly priced.

Live check: Mississippi State fell behind 14-0 early, so the combo's chance is already down to about 3%. That's the other side of combos: one bad quarter in any game sinks the whole thing.

Next: reading injury reports: which "questionable" tags actually move a line.

Market 101 · Lesson 11: Reading injury reports: which "questionable" tags actually move a line

The labels: NFL teams post an injury report on Friday. Out = won't play. Doubtful = very unlikely to play. Questionable = a real toss-up, decided Sunday. About 90 minutes before kickoff, teams post their inactives list, which is the final answer.

Not every injury is worth the same. Rough guide to how much one missing player is worth to the spread:

Who's outTypical line move
Starting QB (replaced by a backup)3 to 7 points
Elite pass rusher or left tackleabout 1 point
Top wide receiver or cornerback0.5 to 1 point
Running back, most other starters0 to 0.5 points

The bigger danger is groups. One missing pass rusher barely moves a line, but several at the same position breaks the unit. That's today's 49ers: Nick Bosa, Mykel Williams and James Thompson Jr. are all out on the defensive line, with Keion White questionable. Without them, SF can't get pressure with four rushers, so it has to blitz more (Football 101, Lesson 6) and leave receivers uncovered. That's why the brief likes Denver at +3.

How to use it on Kalshi:

  1. Check the Friday report for clusters (two or more missing at one spot: offensive line, cornerbacks, pass rushers).
  2. Check the inactives list 90 minutes before kickoff. If a "questionable" star sits, prices can move a few cents in minutes.
  3. If you're betting with the injury news, bet early, before others catch on. If you're betting against it, wait for the inactives so you aren't caught by a surprise.
Market 101 · Lesson 12: Weather, wind and totals: which conditions actually lower scoring

The short version: of all the weather, wind is the one that matters most. Rain and cold sound scary but move scoring much less than people think, unless they're extreme.

Why wind: a football is light and spins. Strong wind (roughly 15+ mph) pushes long passes and kicks off course and knocks them down short. Two things happen:

  1. Deep passes get riskier, so offenses throw short and run more. Short plays take more time and produce fewer big gains.
  2. Long field goals get harder, so drives that would end in 3 points end in punts or 4th-down tries instead.

White dashed arc = the kick in calm air. Yellow arc = the same kick into the wind. Yellow arrows = wind direction. The same thing happens to a deep pass.

The rough guide:

ConditionEffect on scoring
Wind 15+ mphReal drop: fewer deep passes and long field goals. The under gets more likely.
Light or steady rainSmall. Teams still score; fumbles go up a little.
Heavy snow or downpourNoticeable, mainly by slowing passing.
Cold (even below freezing)Small on its own. It matters more combined with wind.
Dome or closed roofNone. Totals in domes tend to be set a bit higher.

Tonight: the Superdome is indoors, so weather plays no part in the 47.5 total. That's part of why the brief calls it a true 50/50.

How to use it on Kalshi:

  1. On game day, check the forecast wind speed for outdoor games, not just "rain."
  2. Totals often drop a point or two once a windy forecast is clear. If you like an under in a windy game, buy early, before the line moves (same idea as injuries in Lesson 11).
  3. Be careful with player yards "over" bets for QBs and deep receivers in strong wind; running backs are affected much less.

Next: following a line from Monday's opening number to Sunday's kickoff.

Market 101 · Lesson 13: Combo math: why you multiply, and when the legs move together

Start with Lesson 1: a Kalshi price is a chance. 51¢ means 51%. A combo contract pays $1 only if every leg wins, so its fair price is the chance that all the legs win.

Why multiply: picture tonight's game played 100 times.

  1. Falcons +1.5 is 51¢, so Atlanta covers in about 51 of the 100 games.
  2. The combo also needs Bijan 70+ yards in those same 51 games. If his chance is 67% no matter what, he gets there in 67% of 51 ≈ 34 games.
  3. 34 of 100 = 34%, so the fair price is 34¢. Multiplying (51% × 67%) is just a shortcut for "a percentage of a percentage."

Each square is one game. White + yellow = the 51 games where the Falcons cover. Yellow = the games where Bijan also gets 70+, which is when the combo pays. Faded = the 49 games the Falcons don't cover.

How price becomes cost: at 34¢, $10 buys about 29.4 contracts, paying $29.41. Kalshi's offer of $24.40 for $10 works out to $10 ÷ $24.40 = 41¢. So you'd be paying 41¢ for something worth 34¢ if the legs were unrelated.

When the legs move together: they aren't unrelated. The games where Atlanta covers are mostly games where its run game worked, and a team that's ahead runs even more to burn the clock (Football 101, Lessons 3 and 11). So within those 51 games, Bijan's chance of 70+ is higher than his overall 67%. The brief estimates about 78%. 78% of 51 ≈ 40 games, or 40%. That's the right-hand grid.

Where 78% comes from: it's a judgment call, not a published number. Reasonable people could say 75% or 82%, which moves the combo between about 38% and 42%. To check a number like this yourself, look at a player's game logs and compare his stats in games his team won or covered vs. games it didn't.

The decision: Kalshi wants 41¢; the brief's best estimate is 40%. No discount, so skip the combo and bet the two legs on their own. It also shows Kalshi already priced the link in: 41¢ is well above the 34¢ "unrelated" price.

The rule: a combo is worth buying only when its price is clearly (about 3¢+) below your estimate of the linked chance. If the legs are unrelated, the multiplied price is the ceiling.

Market 101 · Lesson 14: Following a line from Monday's open to Sunday's kickoff

How a week of betting works: sportsbooks post "opening" lines Sunday night or Monday, with low betting limits. Professional bettors hit the numbers they think are wrong early in the week; books adjust. News (injuries, weather) moves lines any day. Casual money arrives late, mostly on weekends.

Each line is a point spread. Toward the left, the first team is favored; toward the right, the second team. White dot = opening line; yellow dot = Wednesday. Yellow bands = the key numbers 3 and 7 (Market 101, Lesson 3), the most common final margins.

Three kinds of moves, all from this week:

  1. News move (Ravens at Falcons): Lamar Jackson's ankle took the Ravens from 6-point favorites to 3-point underdogs, a 9-point swing. Moves this big only happen for a starting QB (Lesson 11's 3–7 points per QB, and Lamar is worth more than most).
  2. Crossing zero (USC at Penn State): USC -1.5 became Penn State -1.5. The favorite changed. With no injury news, that's usually money on the home team; the efficiency data disagrees with the move, which is why the brief now likes USC more, getting points instead of giving them.
  3. Moving through a key number (Texans at Titans): -3.5 to -7 jumps past 4, 5 and 6 to the next key number. Books are slow to cross 3 and 7 because so many games land there, so a move through one is a strong signal.

How to use it on Kalshi:

  1. If you like a side because of news, bet early, before the line catches up.
  2. If you like the side the public is betting against (like USC this week), wait: popular-side money often pushes your price better by Saturday.
  3. Note the price when you buy and again at kickoff (closing-price tracker, under "How picks are made"). Beating the close is the best sign a pick was good, win or lose.

Next: player props: why "yards" and "touchdowns" are priced so differently.

Market 101 · Lesson 15: Player props: how the yardage ladder is priced, and why touchdowns are different

The yardage ladder: on Kalshi, a player's yards come as a ladder of "X+" contracts (60+, 70+, 80+, ...). Each rung is its own bet. The higher the rung, the less likely it is, so it costs less and pays more.

Yellow bar = Kalshi's price (the market's chance) for each rung before Monday's game. Right column = what $10 pays if it hits. These are the real prices you saw.

How to pick a rung:

  1. Find the sportsbook's single line (Bijan's was 86.5). That's the market's middle: about a 50/50 rung.
  2. Compare it to the player's typical game (his middle game), not his average. Bijan's average was 116 but his typical game was about 83, because one huge game pulled the average up.
  3. Pick the rung where your reason is strongest. A matchup edge (Saints missing run-stoppers) pushes the whole ladder up a bit, which helps the lower rungs most, since they only need a normal game.

Why touchdowns are different: yards pile up all game, so a good player usually gets close to his number. A touchdown is one moment, and it depends on who gets the ball inside the 5 (Football 101, Lesson 17). Monday showed it:

Bijan had more than twice the yards, but Brian Robinson Jr. scored more touchdowns.

Rules of thumb: yards props reward a matchup edge; TD props need a goal-line role. Prices on famous players' TDs carry a "public premium" (Lesson 7), so "Yes, anytime TD" on a star is usually a little expensive.

Market 101 · Lesson 16: Betting splits: "% of bets" vs. "% of money," and how far to trust them

What a split is: some sportsbooks publish two numbers for each side of a bet. % of bets (also called tickets) counts every bet the same, $5 or $5,000. % of money (also called handle) counts dollars. When the two numbers are far apart, they tell you who is on each side.

White bar = share of all bets (tickets) on that side. Yellow bar = share of all dollars (money, or "handle") on that side. Thursday's numbers are from CLEATZ; tonight's and Saturday's are Wednesday snapshots from one sportsbook's data via CLEATZ.

How to read it:

  1. Money % much higher than bets % = a smaller number of bigger bets. Bigger bettors are more often professionals, so books pay attention. Thursday's Bucs moneyline (18% of bets, 53% of money) is the textbook case, and Tampa won outright.
  2. Bets % much higher than money % = lots of small, casual bets. On Saturday's game, USC +1.5 has 59% of bets but only 19% of the money, so the bigger money is on Penn State, against the brief's pick.
  3. Check whether the line moved with the money. USC-Penn State flipped from USC -1.5 to Penn State -1.5: the books moved toward the big money. Once a line has moved, the money is already "in the price." That's why the brief still takes USC at +1.5: it gets the points the move created.
  4. Watch for a move against the money. This week's Browns at Jets: 90% of the money is on Cleveland, yet the line moved from Jets -1 to Jets -2.5. When a book moves the other way, it usually trusts a different, sharper group of bettors more than the crowd (reverse line movement, Lesson 3).

Why not to over-trust splits: each sportsbook only sees its own customers, so one book's split can differ from another's; a single huge bet can make "99% of money" out of one bettor; and early-week numbers change as betting limits rise. That's why the brief's checklist uses splits as a tiebreaker after a football reason, never as the reason. Tonight's Iowa-Washington under has both: a backup QB on a bad offense (the reason) and 99% of the money (the tiebreaker).

On Kalshi: Kalshi doesn't publish splits. Its price already reflects everyone's money, so use sportsbook splits to understand why a Kalshi price is moving, not to predict it.

Market 101 · Lesson 17: Key numbers in totals: why 41, 44 and 51 matter more than 42 or 50

The idea: NFL scores come in chunks: touchdowns with the extra point (7), field goals (3), and sometimes a missed kick or a 2-point try (6 or 8). So combined scores don't spread out evenly. Some finals, like 41 (for example 24-17) and 44 (24-20 or 27-17), happen noticeably more often than their neighbors. Those are the key numbers for totals.

Bar height = roughly how often an NFL game finishes with that many combined points. Yellow bars are the key numbers named in the text (37, 41, 43, 44, 47, 51). These are rounded long-run figures to show the pattern, not exact counts for any season.

Why it matters for your bets: a total line of 41.5 vs. 40.5 is not "one point" in value. The one point between them is 41, one of the most common finals. Under 41.5 wins on a 41-point game; Under 40.5 loses. The same point between 45.5 and 46.5 (covering a 46-point final) is worth much less, because 46 is an ordinary total.

  1. Getting a key number is worth paying for. If Kalshi lists both 40.5 and 41.5 for the same game, under 41.5 costs more, and it should. Pay a few cents for it only when the total crosses 41 or 44; don't pay extra to cross 42 or 45.
  2. Totals that cross key numbers move slower. Books hesitate to move a total from 44.5 to 43.5 (crossing 44). This week's Giants-Commanders total fell from 48.5 to 41.5–42.5 because of heavy rain and wind; it crossed 47, 44 and 43 on the way. If you like the under, the 42.5 version also wins on 41 and 42.
  3. College is weaker on key numbers. College games have more points, more 2-point tries and more blowouts, so combined scores spread out more. Friday's Iowa-Washington finished at 65, nowhere near 41.5, so the key number didn't matter. Key numbers decide close calls; they don't rescue a wrong read.

A real example from this week: Thursday's Bucs-Cowboys total closed at 47.5 to 48.5 (around 47, a key number). It finished at 40. Sunday night's Ravens-Falcons total is 43.5, right between two key numbers (43 and 44). A bet on under 43.5 wins on 43 but loses on 44; a book at 44.5 would win on both.

Connecting the dots: Spreads have their own key numbers, 3 and 7 (the Falcons +2.5 and Bucs +9.5 cards above both mention them), because most margins are a field goal or a touchdown. Totals work the same way, but the pattern is weaker and the key numbers are different (41 and 44 lead). Weather (Lesson 12) and line moves (Lesson 14) tell you which way a total should go; key numbers tell you which line to take once you've decided.

Market 101 · Lesson 18: Middling: when a line moves enough that both sides can win

The idea: if you bet one side early and the line then moves a lot, you can bet the other side at the new number. Most of the time one bet wins and one loses, so you're out only the small extra you paid (on Kalshi, the cents above $1 for the pair). But if the game lands between the two numbers, the "middle," both bets win.

White bars = where each bet wins. Yellow = the middle, where both win: Ravens by 1–3 or Falcons by 1–3. The numbers are tonight's real line move: Ravens -3.5 at the open, Falcons -3 to -3.5 now.

Tonight's real example: Ravens-Falcons opened Ravens -3.5 last Monday. Lamar Jackson was ruled out Friday and the line swung to Falcons -3 to -3.5, about 7 points. Someone who bought Falcons +3.5 on Monday (around 50¢) could now buy Ravens +3.5 (on Kalshi: Falcons wins by over 3.5 → NO, about 52¢). Total cost about $1.02 for a pair that pays at least $1.00:

  1. Game decided by 4 or more either way: one bet pays $1, the other $0. Net: about -2¢.
  2. Game decided by 1, 2 or 3 points either way: both pay. Net: about +98¢.
  3. Why it's worth it here: the middle covers 3, the most common NFL margin (about 1 in 7 games end on exactly 3; Lesson 2). Risking 2¢ to win 98¢ when the chance is well above 2% is a good trade.

Where middles come from: only from movement. Injury news (Lamar tonight), weather (the totals that fell 4–7 points this week in rain and wind), or sharp money. On one day's board, Kalshi already prices every line, so a "same-day middle" (say, "No" on Falcons by over 3.5 plus "Yes" on Falcons by over 1.5) costs about $1.10 for the pair: you lose 10¢ on every miss, and the middle (Falcons by exactly 2 or 3) has to come in more than 1 time in 10 just to break even. That's why you can't build a middle from scratch; you earn it by betting early and being right about where the line was going.

Your own near-miss: Thursday's Bucs went from +10 (Wednesday) to Cowboys -8.5 (Thursday). Bucs +10 early plus Cowboys -8.5 late would have middled on a Dallas win by 9 or 10. Tampa won outright, so only the Bucs side would have paid. Middles are a bonus, not a plan: most lines don't move far enough.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 6 (closing line value) says beating the closing number is the real sign you bet well. A middle is the payoff you can collect from that: when your early number beats the close by more than a field goal, betting the other side at the close turns "I got a good number" into a cheap lottery ticket on the key number.

Market 101 · Lesson 19: Teasers: buying 6 points, and why only some are worth it

The idea: a teaser lets you move the point spread in your favor, usually by 6 points, on two or more games. Every game has to win (it's a parlay, Lesson 4). Underdog +1.5 becomes +7.5; favorite -8.5 becomes -2.5. You get easier bets, but you get paid much less than a normal parlay: a 2-team, 6-point teaser usually pays -120 (risk $12 to win $10).

White bars = the margins where each bet wins. Yellow = the two key numbers, 3 and 7. The 6 points moved the bet from "Chiefs by 9 or more" to "Chiefs by 3 or more," so every Chiefs win by 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 or 8 turned from a loss into a win.

  1. The break-even math: -120 means you need to win 12 of every 22 teasers (54.5%). Both legs have to win, so each leg needs about 74% (because 0.74 × 0.74 ≈ 0.545). A normal spread bet is about 50%; 6 points has to add about 24 percentage points per leg just to break even.
  2. When 6 points is worth 24%: only when the 6 points cross both 3 and 7, the two most common NFL margins (Lesson 2). That's favorites of -7.5 to -8.5 (teased to -1.5 to -2.5) and underdogs of +1.5 to +2.5 (teased to +7.5 to +8.5). Bettors call these "Wong teasers," after the author who found them. Historically they've won about 73–75% per leg, right around break-even at today's prices, which is why books now price them carefully.
  3. When it isn't: teasing a -3 favorite to +3, or a +6 underdog to +12, crosses just one key number (or none). Those 6 points add far less than 24%, so the teaser loses money long term. Totals are worse: points don't cluster as tightly (Lesson 17).
  4. College: margins spread out more and key numbers are weaker, so college teasers rarely pass the 74% test.

Real examples from Week 5: Jaguars -7.5 vs. the Eagles in London teased to -1.5 won (Jacksonville won by 2, on a 57-yard field goal) even though -7.5 lost. Thursday's Cowboys -8.5 teased to -2.5 still lost, because Tampa won outright. A teaser protects you from close calls, not from being wrong about who's better.

On Kalshi: there's no "teaser" button. You can build one in a combo from alternate spread lines (for example, "Yes" on Kansas City wins by over 2.5 plus "No" on Philadelphia wins by over 7.5). Kalshi prices each moved line on its own, so the test is simple: multiply the two prices. If the result is below about 55¢, it's cheaper than a sportsbook's -120 teaser; if it's above, it isn't. Week 6 lines in the teaser range (as of Monday, not picks): Chiefs -8.5 vs. the Chargers, 49ers -7.5 vs. Washington, Panthers +1.5 at the Eagles, Saints +1.5 at the Giants.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 2 says 3 and 7 are the key numbers; Lesson 10 says each extra leg shrinks your odds; Lesson 18 says middles come from crossing key numbers. A teaser is the same idea from the other side: you pay to cross 3 and 7 on purpose. It only makes sense when the line sits right next to both.

Market 101 · Lesson 20: The alternate-line ladder: what each step costs, and where the cheap step is

The idea: Kalshi doesn't list one spread per game; it lists a ladder: "Rams win," "Rams win by over 2.5," "by over 3.5," and so on. Each rung is its own Yes/No market. The trick is to read the gaps between rungs: the drop from one rung to the next is exactly what the market charges for the margins in between.

White bars = Monday-morning Kalshi "Yes" prices on three rungs of the same game (from the brief's MNF table). Yellow = the most expensive step: crossing 3.

  1. Read the gaps: "Rams win" 62¢ minus "by over 2.5" 57¢ = 5¢. That 5¢ is the market's price on the Rams winning by 1 or 2 (about 5%). "By over 2.5" 57¢ minus "by over 3.5" 48¢ = 9¢, the price of the Rams winning by exactly 3.
  2. Why one point cost more than two: 3 is the most common NFL margin (Lesson 2). A single rung that crosses 3 (or 7) is always the expensive step; rungs that cross 4, 5 or 6 are cheap (a few cents each), because few games end there.
  3. Using it as an underdog bettor: on the Bills you could buy "No" on Rams win by over 3.5 at about 52¢ (Bills +3.5), or "No" on Rams win by over 2.5 at about 43¢ (Bills +2.5). The extra 9¢ buys you the Rams-by-exactly-3 outcome. It's worth it only if you think a 3-point Rams win happens more than about 9% of the time. For a game priced near 3, history puts it around 9–10%, so Monday's 9¢ was a fair price, not a bargain.
  4. Where bargains hide: look for a step through 3 or 7 that costs less than usual (say 6–7¢ on a game lined near 3), or a step through 4–6 that costs more than usual (then take the cheaper rung). Thin Kalshi markets misprice single rungs more often than sportsbooks do.

How Monday settled: Bills 37-34 in OT, a Bills win by 3. "No" won on every Rams rung, and "Bills win" paid. Had the Rams kicked the overtime field goal instead (34-37 the other way), "Rams by over 2.5" would have paid and "by over 3.5" wouldn't: the 9¢ rung in action.

This week: Seahawks at Broncos (Thu 5:15 p.m. PT) sits at Seattle -2.5, right next to 3. When the brief prices it Wednesday, it'll compare "Seattle by over 1.5," "over 2.5" and "over 3.5"; a screenshot of Kalshi's ladder from you is the most accurate source.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 15 showed the same ladder for player yards; Lesson 19 (teasers) is paying for several rungs at once; Lesson 18 (middles) is owning two rungs on opposite sides. The ladder is the tool underneath all three.

Next: selling early on Kalshi (cash-outs): when it beats holding, using your three past cash-outs as examples.

Market 101 · Lesson 21: Selling early (cash-outs): when it beats holding, using your three cash-outs

The idea: on Kalshi you can sell a contract before the game ends. Selling isn't free: you get the current bid (what the next buyer will pay right now), which is a little under the ask (what you'd pay to buy), plus Kalshi's fee. So every cash-out costs a few cents per contract. The question is never "am I up or down?" It's "is my honest chance of winning higher or lower than the price I can sell at?"

White bar = what you paid for one "Yes" contract on Bijan Robinson 70+ rushing yards before the Falcons-Saints game. Yellow = roughly what you got when you sold ($9.41 back on $10). Faded bar = what it would have paid at the end (he ran for 145).

  1. The rule: sell when your estimate is below the bid; hold when it's above. What you paid doesn't matter anymore. If you think a player is now 90% to clear his number and the bid is 80¢, holding is worth about 90¢ to you, so selling for 80¢ gives away 10¢.
  2. Good reasons to sell: the reason for the bet changed (a player is ruled out, a QB is benched, the game script flipped), you bought the wrong side by mistake, or you're over your unit limit on one game (Lesson 5).
  3. Bad reasons: the price dipped a few cents and you're nervous, or you want to "lock in" a small profit when your estimate is still higher than the price.
  4. When selling shines: live prices overreact to big plays (Lesson 8). Right after a long touchdown, a "Yes" can jump past its real chance; that's the best time to sell, the same moment that's the worst time to buy (your $20 Bijan 160+ was bought in exactly that spike).

Your three cash-outs (Oct 3–5), graded:

  • Ohio State by 15+, $20 → sold for $18.95 (-$1.05). You'd bought the wrong side (your read was Iowa), so selling was right. Ohio State then won by 17, so the bet you sold would have won. That's fine: judge the decision by its reason, not the result. Keeping a bet you don't believe in is a coin flip you didn't choose.
  • "No" on Bijan 90+, $10 → sold for $9.16 (-$0.84). It went against the brief's read; selling fixed it cheaply. Bijan ran for 145, so holding would have lost all $10.
  • Bijan 70+, $10 at 68¢ → sold for $9.41 (-$0.59), then bought 80+ at 59¢ (won +$6.47). A swap, not an exit: net about +$5.88, versus about +$4.60 from just holding the 70+. It worked, but it was a bigger-risk version of the same bet; the 4¢ round trip is the price of that switch.

Total cost of the three cash-outs: $2.48. Cheap, and two of the three saved real money. That's what a cash-out is for.

This week: if you buy the Broncos +3.5 pick before Wednesday's final injury report and Denver then rules out a third defensive back (Riley Moss), that's a "reason changed" sell. A 2¢ wobble isn't.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 6 (closing line value) asks whether the price moved your way before kickoff; a cash-out is how you collect that move without waiting for the game. Lesson 8 (live betting) is the same overreaction, seen from the selling side.

Next: hedging, buying the other side instead of selling, and when that's cheaper.

Market 101 · Lesson 22: Hedging: buying insurance on a bet you already own

The idea: a hedge is a second bet that wins when your first bet loses. It shrinks your worst case, and it always shrinks your best case too: you pay for insurance whether or not you need it.

First, a Kalshi shortcut: on the same market, buying the other side is the same as selling (Lesson 21). One "Yes" plus one "No" on "Seattle wins by over 3.5" always pays exactly $1, so Kalshi nets them out: owning "No" at 57¢ and then buying "Yes" at 42¢ just locks in $1 for 99¢. The real hedging question is about a different market.

White bars = only the brief's pick: $5 of "No" on "Seattle wins by over 3.5" at 57¢ (8.77 contracts, pays $8.77). Yellow bars = the same pick plus a $3 hedge: "Yes" on "Seattle wins" at 57¢ (5.26 contracts, pays $5.26). Profit is on the $5 or $8 total; fees left out.

  1. What the hedge does: the worst case (Seattle by 4+) shrinks from -$5.00 to -$2.74. The normal good case (Denver wins) drops from +$3.77 to +$0.77. And a bonus appears: if Seattle wins by 1, 2 or 3, both bets win, +$6.03 (a small middle, Lesson 18).
  2. The cost: every hedge buys a price with Kalshi's fee and the market's margin built in. Hedge every bet and you pay that toll twice, every time; over a season it eats the edge the first bet was supposed to have. If you want to hedge a bet the minute you place it, the bet was too big: bet less instead (Lesson 5).
  3. When a hedge is worth it: (a) a combo with one leg left, when the payout is big compared with what you put in; (b) news changed your estimate (an injury, a QB change) and selling is expensive or impossible; (c) the bet has grown into more than you're comfortable losing.
  4. Your USC + Over combo is the textbook case. $14.99 to win $55.24; at halftime (17-17) the USC leg looked good, so everything rode on the Over 55.5. Suppose the Over was then about 60¢ (an example; the real halftime price wasn't captured): $12 of "No" at 40¢ pays $30. With USC covering, the 54-point finish becomes +$3.01 instead of -$14.99, and an Over would have paid +$28.25 instead of +$40.25. You give up $12 of upside to remove an $18 swing.
  5. Hedge or sell? If you can sell the same market, selling is the hedge and costs one fee, not two. Use a different-market hedge only when there's no clean exit, like a combo (you can sell one, but its bid is often thin).

This week: if you take Broncos +3.5 tonight, the brief's plan is no hedge: it's a ¼-unit bet, small enough to ride either way. The diagram shows why: the hedge turns a +$3.77 win into +$0.77.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 18 (middles) is a hedge that can win twice; Lesson 21 (cash-outs) is the same-market version; Lesson 13 (combo legs that move together) explains why combos make the best hedge candidates.

Next: reading your own results: how to tell good decisions from good luck after three weeks of bets.

Market 101 · Lesson 23: Good decision or good luck? Reading your own results

The idea: a bet's result mixes two things: the decision (was the price wrong in your favor?) and luck (how the game bounced). One game can't separate them. A good bet loses about 45% of the time; a bad one wins plenty. So you grade the decision with things you can check before kickoff, and let the win-loss record catch up over hundreds of bets.

Four real bets from the last three weeks, sorted by decision (rows) and result (columns). "Beat the close" = the price moved your way between buying and kickoff (Lesson 6). Yellow = what to do about it.

  1. Why the record is mostly noise so far: the brief is 24-21 (-0.37 units) after Thursday. If every pick were a pure coin flip, 45 picks would land between about 19 and 26 wins two times out of three. 24-21 is inside that range, so the record alone proves nothing yet, good or bad. To show that a 55% bettor beats 50% with real confidence takes roughly 400 bets.
  2. The faster test: the closing price. The market's price at kickoff is the best estimate there is. Beat it again and again and you are probably finding value, whatever the scoreboard says. The brief's tracker (under How picks are made) has 16 rows: 5 clearly beat the close (your Falcons +2.5, your Bucs +9.5, Kyren 70+, Broncos +3.5, and Penn State's move from 6 to 6.5 so far), 1 clearly didn't (your Over 55.5), the rest about even. That's a decent sign, still a small sample.
  3. The four boxes: good decision, won = do it again. Good decision, lost = a bad beat; change nothing (Kyren lost because the Rams trailed, but the price moved our way before kickoff). Weak decision, won = luck; the dangerous box, because winning makes it feel smart. Weak decision, lost = the leak: the live Bijan 160+ bet (bought right after a big play, Lesson 8) cost $20.
  4. Your three weeks, sorted this way: Sept 24–28 -$39.11, Oct 1–5 -$36.25, Oct 8–12 -$1.24 (total -$76.60). The losses are not spread evenly: brief picks you bet as written made +$13.75 last week, while your own add-ons (long combos, live long shots) lost $19.12 the week before and $14.99 last week. The trend is improving as more of your money goes to bets with a written reason.
  5. A habit that makes this easy: before you tap Buy, write one sentence: "the price is wrong because ___." If you can't fill the blank, it's a weak-row bet, win or lose. Send the sentence with your screenshot and the brief will sort each bet into a box at the weekly wrap.

This week: Thursday's Broncos +3.5 is a top-left bet (a reason, beat the close, won). Saturday's Penn State +6.5 has a written reason (Michigan's line injuries) and the line already moved our way; if it loses, it's still a top-row bet.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 5 (units) keeps you alive long enough for the record to mean something; Lesson 6 (closing line value) is the early grade; Lesson 21 (cash-outs) and Lesson 8 (live betting) are where most weak-row bets come from.

Next: a weekly betting budget: how much to set aside for Thursday–Monday, and how to split it between singles and combos.

Market 101 · Lesson 24: A weekly budget: how much to set aside for Thursday–Monday, and how to split it

The idea: decide the whole week's money before Thursday night, and every bet comes out of that pot. The budget is the most you can lose in a week, so a bad Saturday can't turn into a desperate Sunday night. Units (Lesson 5) say how big one bet is; the budget says how many bets the week can hold.

Your two finished weeks with tracked bets and the suggested budget, all drawn on the same dollar scale. White = singles with a written reason (brief picks), yellow = your own combos and long shots.

  1. What your weeks looked like: Oct 1–5 you bet $174.99 (about 9 units) on 16 bets and lost $36.25; $74.99 of it was your own picks, mostly combos and live long shots. Oct 8–12 you bet $34.97 on 3 bets and lost only $1.24; the two brief picks made +$13.75 and your combo lost $14.99. Smaller weeks with a reason behind each bet lost far less.
  2. The suggested budget: $60 a week (3 units of $20). At the brief's sizes (¼ unit = $5, ½ unit = $10) that covers 6 to 10 bets, more than the brief usually recommends. This week so far the brief's picks add up to $15 (Broncos $5, which won; Penn State $5; Bijan $5).
  3. The split: about two-thirds singles. $40 for single bets with a written reason (Lesson 23's "the price is wrong because ___" sentence), $15 for combos (three at $5 at most, because combos lose more often: Lesson 13), $5 for fun (a live bet or long shot you just want to sweat). When a slice is spent, it's spent; don't borrow from singles to buy another combo.
  4. The stop rule: when the $60 is gone, you're done until next Thursday. Winnings don't refill the same week; a simple habit is to set half of any profit aside at the Monday wrap. Chasing a loss on Monday night with fresh money is how a -$10 week becomes -$40 (the live Bijan 160+ bet, $20 right after a big play, was that kind of bet).
  5. Why it helps the math too: the record only becomes meaningful over hundreds of bets (Lesson 23). A steady weekly budget keeps you in the game long enough for good decisions to show up, instead of one big week deciding everything.

This week: you've logged $0 so far. If you follow the brief's two remaining picks at its size, that's $10 of a $60 week, leaving plenty of room without stretching. If you'd rather use a different number (say $40 or $100), tell the brief and the tracker at the top will show what's left each day.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 5 (units) sizes each bet; Lesson 13 (combos) explains why combos get the smaller slice; Lesson 21 (cash-outs) and Lesson 8 (live betting) are where most budget leaks start.

Next: reading a Kalshi contract's rules line: what counts (overtime, ties, postponed games, a player who doesn't play).

Market 101 · Lesson 25: Read the rules line: what counts (overtime, ties, a player who doesn't play)

The idea: every Kalshi contract has a "Rules" section under the price. It says exactly what has to happen for YES to pay, which stats source decides it, and what happens in the odd cases. Two bets that look identical, one at a sportsbook and one on Kalshi, can settle differently in those odd cases. Reading the rules takes 20 seconds and is the only way to know what you actually bought.

Four situations, two places to bet. The Kalshi column for players comes from Kalshi's help page on NFL player markets; the sportsbook column is the usual house rule (each book prints its own, so check yours).

  1. Overtime counts (top row). Both sportsbooks and Kalshi's game markets include overtime for spreads and totals. This week it mattered twice: UAB-East Carolina was 17-17 (34 points) after regulation and finished 30-17 in overtime, 47 points, still under 47.5 by half a point. Denver-Seattle was 14-14 and the Broncos' 53-yard field goal in OT is what made Thursday's Broncos +3.5 a win "outright" instead of a cover.
  2. Ties (second row). On Oct 11 the Giants and Commanders tied 27-27. On Kalshi, "Washington wins" and "New York G wins" both settled NO (nobody won), and "Washington wins by over 3.5" settled NO, so the brief's Giants +3.5 won. At a sportsbook, a two-way moneyline on a tie is usually refunded and the spread is graded on a 0-point margin. Same game, different payout: if you hold "Team X wins → NO" in a game that can end tied, a tie is a win for you on Kalshi.
  3. A player who never plays (third row). Kalshi's help page says that if a player is active but never takes a snap, a standard NFL player market "settles at the fair price before game start" (a price Kalshi sets, not what you paid). Sportsbooks usually void the bet and refund you. So if you bought "Bijan Robinson: 70+ rushing yards" at 66¢ and he were active but never touched the field, you'd get back roughly whatever the market thought before kickoff, not 66¢ and not $0.
  4. A player ruled out before the game (bottom row). The help page doesn't spell this case out, so the contract's own rules decide. This is exactly why the brief checks inactives 90 minutes before kickoff (checklist #9): the cleanest way to avoid a weird settlement is to know who's playing before you buy.
  5. The stats source. Each rule names where the number comes from (the official box score). Live pages can lag or be wrong: on Oct 11 a stats feed showed Bijan at 38 yards at halftime while Kalshi's own tracker had 81. The contract pays on the official number, not on whatever page you're refreshing. Replay rulings count too: Saturday, Indiana's Nick Marsh caught a would-be go-ahead touchdown on 4th-and-4, but replay showed his left foot out, so anything riding on that catch never happened.

Your 20-second check before you tap Buy: (1) Does it include overtime? (2) What happens on a tie? (3) For a player market: what if he doesn't play? (4) Which stats source settles it? If any answer surprises you, the price may not mean what you think.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 1 turned prices into chances; this lesson makes sure the "event" behind the chance is the one you think it is. Lesson 18 (middling) and Lesson 20 (the ladder) both depend on exact settlement rules at a key number, and Lesson 26 in Football 101 explains how NFL and college overtime work.

Next: why the same game can have a different "favorite" on Kalshi and at the books (it happened all week in Saints-Giants), and how to tell which number to trust.

Market 101 · Lesson 26: Two different favorites: when Kalshi and the books disagree on who's supposed to win

The idea: a sportsbook and Kalshi can list different "favorites" for the same game, and the favorite can flip during the week. That sounds like a contradiction, but in a close game it usually isn't one. Both prices are estimates of the same thing, the chance each team wins. Convert both to a percentage, take out the house's cut, and compare. If both land near 50%, "favorite" is just a label on a coin flip.

Two number lines of win chance, 44% to 60%. Yellow = the sportsbook number with the cut removed; white square = the exchange price; the shaded band from 48% to 52% is the coin-flip zone.

  1. Turn the book's moneylines into a chance and remove the cut (top line, "Book 52.2%"). Tonight, DraftKings had Green Bay -120 / Dallas +100 (Oct 15). -120 means 120 / 220 = 54.5%; +100 means 100 / 200 = 50.0%. Those add to 104.5%, and the extra 4.5% is the cut (Lesson 1). Divide each by the total: Green Bay 54.5 / 104.5 = 52.2%.
  2. Read the exchange the same way ("Market 52.5%"). A prediction market had Green Bay 53¢ and Dallas 48¢ (Oct 16, SI). Those add to 101¢; dividing out the extra cent gives Green Bay about 52.5%. Use the middle of the Yes and No prices, not just the last trade, and remember Kalshi's fee comes out of your payout (Lesson 7). The two places agree within half a point.
  3. Why the label flips (bottom line). Saints at Giants opened Giants -2.5, moved to Saints -1.5, then back to pick'em or Giants -1.5 by Saturday, while Kalshi sat at 50/50. Near a pick'em, each point of spread is worth roughly 3 percentage points of win chance, so "Giants -1.5" is about 54% and "Saints -1.5" about 46%: the whole week's flip-flopping stayed between 46% and 54%. The Saints won 34-27. Neither "favorite" was wrong; it was a close game priced as one.
  4. When a disagreement matters. If the two prices are 3¢ (3 points) or more apart after removing the cut, one of them is probably slow (checklist #5). Look for the reason: an injury the books have priced and the exchange hasn't, or the reverse. Then buy the cheaper side only if your reason agrees with it.
  5. Which one to trust. Big sportsbooks move first on injury news because sharp bettors hit them; Kalshi often catches up within minutes, but thin games can lag. For the brief's picks, the book's no-cut number is the starting point (Lesson 1), and Kalshi is where you buy if its price is at or below the brief's limit.

Your 30-second check: (1) Convert both prices to percentages. (2) Remove the cut (divide each side by the total). (3) If both are between 48% and 52%, ignore the word "favorite." (4) If they're 3+ points apart, ask why before you buy.

Connecting the dots: this is Lesson 1 (implied probability) applied to two markets at once, and Lesson 7 (Vegas vs. Kalshi gaps) with the cut removed. Lesson 14 (following a line all week) explains why a favorite can flip on news, and Lesson 17's key numbers explain why a move from -1.5 to -2.5 matters less than one from -2.5 to -3.

Next: fading the public: what "86% of the money" on the 49ers really tells you, and when to ignore it.

Market 101 · Lesson 27: Fading the public: what "83% of the money on the 49ers" tells you, and when to ignore it

The idea: "fading the public" means betting against the side most bettors are on. Lesson 16 explained the two numbers books publish: % of bets (how many tickets) and % of money (how many dollars). This lesson is what to do with them. Short version: a lopsided crowd is not, by itself, a reason to bet the other side. What matters is whether the price moves with the crowd or against it.

Tonight's Commanders at 49ers splits (SportsBettingDime, Mon 11:30 a.m. PT). White bar = share of tickets; yellow bar = share of money; the dashed line is 100%.

  1. Read the bars (top row, "49ers -7.5"). 79% of spread tickets and 83% of the money are on San Francisco. That says the 49ers are popular, which a 5-0 team on national TV usually is. On its own, it says nothing about who covers.
  2. Watch which way the price moves. If nearly everyone bets San Francisco, a book that wanted balance would move toward San Francisco (to -8, or -7.5 at -120). Instead, the Washington side got more expensive (-110 → -115) and Circa went from -8 to -7.5. When the price moves against the side with most of the money, the book is respecting the bets on the other side more. Bettors call this "reverse line movement."
  3. Find the big bets (bottom row, "Washington to win"). Washington has 2% of moneyline tickets but 12% of the money: 12 ÷ 2 = 6. San Francisco has 88% of the money on 98% of the tickets: 88 ÷ 98 ≈ 0.9. So the average Washington bet is about 6 ÷ 0.9 ≈ 6.7 times bigger than the average San Francisco bet. Many small bets on the favorite, a few large ones on the underdog.
  4. When the price moves with the crowd, you pay more (bottom note). Sunday night every preview liked the Cowboys-Packers Over, and the total climbed from 51.5 to 53.5. Anyone betting the Over at 53.5 needed 2 more points than Wednesday's bettors for the same guess. Final: 36 points. One game proves nothing, but it's why the brief passed (checklist #4). The middle row ("Over 52") is tonight's version of the same thing: the total opened 50.5.
  5. Keep score honestly. The brief's splits scorecard (Trends section): the Bucs moneyline with 18% of bets but 53% of money won; the Iowa-Washington Under with 99% of the money lost by 23 points. Splits are a tiebreaker after a football reason, never the reason.

Your 30-second check: (1) Is one side above about 75% of bets? That's popularity, not information. (2) Did the line, or the price on it, move toward that side or away from it? Away means bigger bettors are on the other side. (3) On the unpopular side, is % of money bigger than % of bets? Divide the two to see how much bigger those bets are. (4) Act only if you also have a football reason (an injury, a matchup).

Connecting the dots: Lesson 3 (why lines move) and Lesson 14 (following a line all week) explain the moves; Lesson 16 defined the two percentages; Lesson 6 (closing line value) is how to check later whether the big bettors were right: if Washington closes at +7 tonight, the +7.5 the brief took beat the close.

Next: short weeks and Thursday nights: does rest really change the score? (Patriots at Bears, Fri 9:15 a.m. JST / Thu 5:15 p.m. PT.)

How it played out: the price moved toward Washington while 83% of the money sat on San Francisco; the 49ers won by 6, so Commanders +7.5 (and the bigger bettors) won.

Market 101 · Lesson 28: Short weeks and Thursday nights: does rest really change the score?

The idea: a Thursday game comes three days after the teams' Sunday games, instead of the normal six. The popular theory is that tired, under-prepared teams play sloppy, simple football, so Thursday games score less and you should bet the Under. Some of that is real; the trouble is that everyone has heard it, so the price may already include it. This lesson is how to tell.

Top: prep days (yellow = this week's Thursday teams). Bottom: white dot = the market's total, yellow dot = points actually scored (or, for this Thursday, CBS SportsLine's projection, dashed). Open white circle = where the Patriots-Bears total opened.

  1. Count the days (top bars). A normal week is 6 days between games. A Monday-night team playing Sunday gets 5 (the 49ers this week, who also fly to Atlanta). A Thursday team gets 3. Coaches say the first thing that goes is the game plan: fewer new plays, more of what the team already does well. Practices are mostly walk-throughs, which is why early-week injury reports for Thursday games say "estimated."
  2. Check whether the rest is equal. This Thursday both teams played at home Sunday, so both have 3 days; New England also travels. A rest gap (one team off a bye, the other off a Monday game) matters more than both teams being equally short.
  3. Look at what's happened (bottom dots). This season's two Thursday games the brief tracked both finished well under the market's total: Bucs-Cowboys 40 points against about 48, Broncos-Seahawks 31 (with overtime) against 40.5. That supports the theory, but two games is a tiny sample: a couple of high-scoring Thursdays would erase it. Treat it as a reason to look, not a reason to bet.
  4. See which way the number moved (bottom row). If the crowd believed "Thursday = Under," the total would drift down. Patriots-Bears did the opposite: it opened 49.5 and is 50.5 now, even though CBS SportsLine's model projects about 44 points and Covers' writer takes the Under. When the total moves up while the sharper sources like the Under, you're getting a slightly better number for the Under than the opener (Lesson 14), which is the good kind of disagreement.
  5. Find the football reason. Rest alone never makes a pick (checklist #2). Here the candidate is New England's offensive line, missing several starters against Chicago's blitz-heavy pass rush: a short week gives a patched-up line less time to practice together. Wednesday's injury report names who's out; that's what turns the lean into a pick or a pass.

Your 30-second check: (1) How many days does each team get, and is it equal? (2) Who travels? (3) Has the total moved, and which way? (4) Is there a specific injury or matchup that a short week makes worse? Only with (4) is it a bet.

Connecting the dots: Lesson 3 and Lesson 14 explain line moves; Lesson 11 (reading injury reports) matters more on a short week, since "estimated" practice reports hide who really practiced; Lesson 17 (key numbers in totals) applies here too: 51 is a common final total, so Under 50.5 loses on exactly 51.

How it played out: Wednesday's report named the injuries (four New England guards gone, three cornerbacks out), and the brief turned the lean into a ¼-unit Under 50.5 pick; result in Friday's edition.

Lines and injuries move constantly; check your book or exchange before placing anything. Leans are informed opinions, not guarantees. If gambling stops being fun, call 1-800-GAMBLER.
